       13 +## Findings (researched 2026-09-25)                           
       14 +                                                              
       15 +### Context that drives the decisions                         
       16 +                                                              
       17 +- **Workload**: a small RAG prototype (3–5 KB articles), one c
          +hat endpoint, citations, `docker compose up` for the reviewer.
          + Most of the work is orchestration (ingest → chunk → embed → r
          +etrieve → prompt → cite), not heavy compute.                  
       18 +- **Dev machine**: 4 CPU cores, 15 GB RAM, **no GPU**, Ollama 
          +not installed yet. The local model must run acceptably on CPU.
          + The reviewer's machine is unknown, so we assume CPU too.     
       19 +- **Planned features in the README**: multi-language Q&A, resp
          +onse history, a statistics menu, polite refusal or human hando
          +ff, and speed and performance tests. These affect the model an
          +d embedding choices.                                          
       20 +                                                              
       21 +### 1. Backend: Python vs Node.js → **Python (FastAPI)**      
       22 +                                                              
       23 +| Criterion | Python + FastAPI | Node.js + Express/NestJS |   
       24 +|---|---|---|                                                 
       25 +| RAG/ML ecosystem | Best in class: official `ollama` and `ant
          +hropic` SDKs, numpy, LangChain/LlamaIndex, ChromaDB, sentence-
          +transformers, eval tooling | Good, but smaller, and many RAG l
          +ibraries are Python-first ports |                             
       26 +| Vector math (cosine similarity) | numpy, one line, vectorise
          +d | Manual loops or extra libraries |                         
       27 +| API design | Pydantic request/response models, auto-generate
          +d OpenAPI/Swagger at `/docs`, native async, SSE streaming | Si
          +milar with NestJS + class-validator, more boilerplate |       
       28 +| Testing | pytest + FastAPI `TestClient`/httpx, easy to mock 
          +the LLM | Jest/Vitest, also fine |                            
       29 +| Brief's recommendation | Explicitly recommended ("C#/.NET or
          + Python") | Allowed |                                         
       30 +| One language across the stack | No (TS on the frontend) | Ye
          +s, the main advantage of Node |                               
       31 +                                                              
       32 +**Decision: Python 3.12 + FastAPI.**                          
       33 +- **For**: the strongest RAG ecosystem, numpy for the in-memor
          +y vector store, free OpenAPI docs (helps the "API design" grad
          +ing), clean async streaming, and the language the brief recomm
          +ends.                                                         
       34 +- **Against / trade-off**: two languages in the repo. This is 
          +acceptable because the frontend is thin.                      
       35 +- **Why not Node.js**: its only real advantage is sharing Type
          +Script with the frontend, and that doesn't outweigh the weaker
          + ML and vector tooling.                                       
       36 +- **Orchestration: raw SDKs, not LangChain/LlamaIndex.** The p
          +ipeline is about 200 lines. Writing it directly makes every st
          +ep easy to explain in the technical interview ("walk us throug
          +h any block of code"). It avoids a large, fast-changing depend
          +ency and keeps full control over the prompt, the citation form
          +at, and the refusal behaviour. Semantic Kernel is .NET-centric
          + and doesn't apply.                                           
       37 +                                                              
       38 +### 2. Frontend: React vs Next.js vs Vite + TypeScript → **Vit
          +e + React + TypeScript + TailwindCSS**                        
       39 +                                                              
       40 +These options are not mutually exclusive. **React** is the UI 
          +library. **Next.js** is a full-stack framework built on React.
          + **Vite** is a build tool/dev server that commonly hosts a Rea
          +ct app. Similarly, **TypeScript vs TailwindCSS is not an eithe
          +r/or choice**: TypeScript is the programming language (type sa
          +fety), and Tailwind is a CSS styling framework. The recommenda
          +tion is to use **both**.

       42 +| Option | For | Against |                                    
       43 +|---|---|---|

  ⎿  Updated documentation/taskdocs/storage.md (+46 -1)
       3  ## Description
       4  
       5  1. Based on documentation/taskdocs/ the backend should ingest a
           small set of provided text documents (you should create 3-5 mo
          ck enterprise knowledge base articles), chunk them, store them 
          in a local or in-memory vector store, and expose a chat endpoin
          t. Make a research which approach will be better for our goal.
       6 -2. Document in this file your findings with explonation why we 
         -should, or should not use the approach.                        
       6   No newline at end of file
       7 +2. Document in this file your findings with explonation why we 
         +should, or should not use the approach.                        
       8 +                                                               
       9 +---                                                            
      10 +                                                               
      11 +## Findings (researched 2026-09-25)                            
      12 +                                                               
      13 +### Sizing the problem first                                   
      14 +                                                               
      15 +- 3–5 mock articles of about 1–3 pages each give **roughly 30–1
         +00 chunks** at 300–500 tokens per chunk.                       
      16 +- With 768-dim float32 embeddings (`embeddinggemma`), that is *
         +*< 0.5 MB of vectors**.                                        
      17 +- A brute-force cosine similarity search over 100 vectors takes
         + **microseconds** in numpy, and it is *exact* (no approximate-N
         +N recall loss).                                                
      18 +- Scale is therefore not a factor for choosing the store. The d
         +eciding factors are simplicity, restart behaviour, explainabili
         +ty, and a clean path to production.                            
      19 +                                                               
      20 +### Options compared                                           
      21 +                                                               
      22 +| Option | Type | For | Against |                              
      23 +|---|---|---|---|                                              
      24 +| **In-memory numpy (custom)** | In-memory | No extra dependenc
         +y or container, exact results, about 60 lines of fully explaina
         +ble code, trivial to unit test, fastest for small corpora | Los
         +t on restart, so embeddings must be recomputed (slow on CPU). N
         +o metadata filtering or ANN index. Single-process only |       
      25 +| ChromaDB (embedded, `PersistentClient`) | Local, on disk | Pe
         +rsistence, metadata filters, suggested in the brief, runs in-pr
         +ocess | Heavy dependency tree, frequent breaking API changes, a
         +nd a black box to explain. Brings no benefit at ~100 chunks |  
      26 +| ChromaDB (server container) | Local service | Same as above, 
         +plus a separate service | An extra container and network hop fo
         +r nothing at this size |                                       
      27 +| pgvector (Postgres in Docker) | Local service | Production-gr
         +ade, SQL + metadata + HNSW index, persists | An extra container
         +, schema/migrations, slower startup. Overkill for a prototype |
      28 +| FAISS / LanceDB / Qdrant | Embedded/service | Fast ANN at mil
         +lions of vectors | Built for scale we don't have. Extra depende
         +ncy |                                                          
      29 +                                                               
      30 +### Decision: **in-memory vector store + on-disk embedding cach
         +e, behind a `VectorStore` interface**                          
      31 +                                                               
      32 +1. **`InMemoryVectorStore`**: a numpy matrix of L2-normalised e
         +mbeddings plus a parallel list of chunk metadata (`doc_id`, `ti
         +tle`, `section`, `chunk_id`, `text`). Search is a single matrix
         +-vector dot product followed by top-k. A **similarity threshold
         +** is also applied: if the best score is below it, the backend 
         +replies that "the documentation doesn't cover this" instead of 
         +letting the LLM guess. This is the basis for the polite refusal
         + / human handoff feature.                                      
      33 +2. **Embedding cache** (fixes the restart downside): after inge
         +stion, vectors and metadata are saved to `data/index/index.npz`
         + + `meta.json` on a Docker volume. The key is a hash of *(docum
         +ent contents + chunking parameters + embedding model name)*. On
         + startup, if the hash matches, the index loads instantly. Other
         +wise it is re-ingested automatically. Changing a doc or the mod
         +el therefore rebuilds the index, with no stale data.           
      34 +3. **Interface** (`add`, `search`, `count`): Chroma or pgvector
         + can be swapped in later without touching the RAG pipeline. Thi
         +s is the trade-off story for the README and the interview: "exa
         +ct in-memory search is the right tool at prototype scale; here 
         +is the seam where pgvector would plug in at 100k+ chunks or mul
         +ti-replica deployment."                                        
      35 +                                                               
      36 +**Why not Chroma or pgvector now**: they add a container or hea
         +vy dependency, startup time and failure modes, but they don't i
         +mprove retrieval quality at this size. Brute-force search is *m
         +ore* accurate than their ANN indexes. We should move to pgvecto
         +r when any of these holds: the corpus grows beyond ~50–100k chu
         +nks, several backend replicas need a shared index, or we need i
         +ncremental updates and metadata-filtered queries (e.g. per-prod
         +uct or per-customer ACLs).                                     
      37 +                                                               
      38 +### Chunking approach (part of ingestion)                      
      39 +                                                               
      40 +- The KB articles are written as **Markdown** with a stable fro
         +nt-matter `id`/`title`. This gives citations a human-readable t
         +itle and section.                                              
      41 +- **Structure-aware chunking**: split on headings (`##`/`###`) 
         +first, then split long sections into windows of about 400 token
         +s with about 60 tokens of overlap. Each chunk keeps `doc_id`, `
         +title`, and `section` (the heading path), so a citation can say
         + *"Admin Guide › SSO Configuration"* rather than just a file na
         +me.

● I've finished all three steps in steps.md and added the findings to the task
  files. I also ticked the three research items in the README checklist. I
  haven't committed anything.

  Step 1: goal.md. It only states the goal: a RAG chatbot that answers CSMs'
  questions with citations, using only internal documentation. There was nothing
  to do beyond reading it.

  Step 2: research.md. The findings and reasoning are appended to the file.
  - Backend: Python with FastAPI. Python has the best RAG and vector-maths
    tools, the brief recommends it, and FastAPI generates the API docs for free.
    I suggest calling the model SDKs directly instead of using LangChain, so
    every block of code is easy to explain in the interview.
  - Frontend: Vite + React + TypeScript + TailwindCSS. Next.js would duplicate
    the backend we already have.
  - Local model: your machine has 4 CPU cores, 15 GB RAM and no GPU, and Ollama
    isn't installed yet. So the model has to be about 4B parameters or smaller:
    - Chat model: gemma3:4b, with qwen3:4b as the alternative to compare
      against.
    - Embedding model: embeddinggemma, which supports many languages, so it
      covers your planned multi-language feature.
    - Anthropic has no embeddings API, so embeddings always run locally in
      Ollama, even when Claude answers the questions.
  - Claude:
    - Main option: your own API key in ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, which is what the
      brief asks for.
    - Subscription: since June 2026, Pro and Max plans include a monthly credit
      for Claude's agent library (the Agent SDK). But Anthropic doesn't allow
      apps to offer a "log in with your Claude subscription" option to their
      users. The subscription can only be an optional setup for your own
      personal testing.

  Step 3: storage.md.
  - In-memory vector store. The 5 articles come to about 100 chunks, so a simple
    exact search in memory takes microseconds.
  - Saved to disk. The vectors are saved so a restart doesn't recompute them on
    a slow CPU. They are rebuilt automatically when a document or the embedding
    model changes.
  - Easy to swap later. The store sits behind a small interface, so pgvector can
    replace it if the project grows.
  - Refusal threshold. A minimum similarity score decides when the documentation
    doesn't cover a question. That supports your polite-refusal /
    hand-off-to-a-human item.
  - Articles. I also proposed chunking by Markdown heading and 5 topics for the
    mock articles.

  Things that weren't clear:
  1. research.md compares options that aren't really alternatives. React vs
     Next.js vs Vite: Vite is the build tool that runs React. TypeScript vs
     Tailwind: one is the language, the other is the styling, and the
     recommendation is to use both.
  2. "Claude with subscription": do you want the optional subscription setup for
     your own testing, or is the API key enough?
  3. The README recommends C#/.NET, but the task only compared Python and Node,
     so I left .NET out.

  Questions before the next phase:
  - Are the 5 proposed article topics OK?
  - Should I install Ollama locally, or only run it through Docker Compose?
  - Should I commit these documentation changes?

      22 +## Future-readiness design (added 2026-09-25)                  
      23 +                                                               
      24 +> **Status**: none of the ideas above are implemented yet, and 
         +none are marked done. This section records the **seams** that t
         +he first version (MVP) of the code, storage and project structu
         +re must include, so that each idea can be added later **without
         + refactoring**. Each seam is marked **MVP** (build it now, chea
         +ply) or **Later** (only reserve room for it).                  
      25 +                                                               
      26 +### Cross-cutting design rules for the MVP                     
      27 +                                                               
      28 +1. **Every chat turn produces a structured result, not just tex
         +t.** The RAG pipeline returns a `ChatResult` containing `answer
         +`, `citations`, `language`, `provider`, `model`, `usage` (input
         +/output tokens), `timings` (retrieval_ms, time-to-first-token, 
         +total_ms), `top_score`, and `refused` (bool). The MVP only logs
         + it. Stats, costs, history and performance tabs will later just
         + *persist and read* it.                                        
      29 +2. **The API is versioned and forward-compatible.** Routes live
         + under `/api/v1/…`. `ChatRequest` = `{ message, conversation_id
         +?, options?: { language?, detail?, provider? } }`. Optional fie
         +lds can be added later without breaking clients. Responses alwa
         +ys carry `conversation_id` and `message_id`.                   
      30 +3. **Two separate provider interfaces**: `LLMProvider` (chat) a
         +nd `EmbeddingProvider` (vectors), each resolved through a **reg
         +istry** from configuration. They are never imported directly by
         + the pipeline.                                                 
      31 +4. **Two separate kinds of storage**:                          
      32 +   - the **vector index** (derived data, rebuildable, in-memory
         + + npz cache; see `storage.md`)                                
      33 +   - an **operational database** (source of truth for events, h
         +istory, costs), which is **added later** as SQLite behind a rep
         +ository interface.                                             
      34 +5. **The "hidden" UI is a real admin area, not just hidden.**  
      35 +   - Backend: all admin endpoints under `/api/v1/admin/*`, guar
         +ded by an `ADMIN_TOKEN` env var (disabled when unset).         
      36 +   - Frontend: an `/admin` route opened by a keyboard shortcut.
         + Hiding a menu is not security, so the token is what protects t
         +he data.                                                       
      37 +                                                               
      38 +### Idea → seam mapping                                        
      39 +                                                               
      40 +| # | Idea | What the MVP must already have | What gets added l
         +ater |                                                         
      41 +|---|---|---|---|                                              
      42 +| 1 | Hidden statistics menu | `ChatResult` metrics (rule 1). A
         + single `record_event(result)` hook called after every turn, a 
         +no-op/log-only implementation in the MVP | SQLite `chat_events`
         + table plus `/api/v1/admin/stats` (questions/day, refusal rate,
         + avg latency, top cited docs, provider split) and a Stats tab |
      43 +| 2 | Hidden costs menu + optimisation | Providers **return tok
         +en usage**: Anthropic `usage.input_tokens/output_tokens`, Ollam
         +a `prompt_eval_count/eval_count`. `usage` is included in `ChatR
         +esult` | A pricing table in config (`$ per MTok` per model; Oll
         +ama = $0 or an estimated compute cost), a Costs tab, and optimi
         +sations: Anthropic **prompt caching** of the system prompt, few
         +er/shorter chunks (top-k tuning), routing simple questions to H
         +aiku, and a **semantic answer cache** (reuse the `VectorStore` 
         +interface on past questions) |                                 
      44 +| 3 | Professional, concise answers; detail on request | Prompt
         +s live in **versioned template files** (`backend/app/prompts/*.
         +md`), not inline strings. The system prompt already enforces a 
         +professional, concise, cited style | An `options.detail = "conc
         +ise" \| "detailed"` field chooses the template variant, plus a 
         +"More details" button in the UI that re-asks with `detail=detai
         +led` |                                                         
      45 +| 4 | Q&A in different languages | **Multilingual embedding mod
         +el** (`embeddinggemma`, already chosen), so questions in other 
         +languages still retrieve English docs. `language` field in `Cha
         +tResult`. Chunk metadata includes `lang` | `options.language` (
         +`auto` by default = reply in the question's language), a langua
         +ge selector in the UI, and optionally KB articles in other lang
         +uages (filtered via `lang` metadata) |                         
      46 +| 5 | Hidden response-history tab | `conversation_id` + `messag
         +e_id` in the API from day 1. The frontend keeps the conversatio
         +n in state | SQLite `conversations` + `messages` tables (questi
         +on, answer, citations JSON, provider, timings) via a `HistoryRe
         +pository`, a History tab, and multi-turn context (question rewr
         +iting using earlier turns) |                                   
      47 +| 6 | Hidden tab with unit, speed and performance tests | The *
         +*evaluation set is data**: `backend/tests/eval/questions.yaml` 
         +(question → expected `doc_id`s / must-refuse). The benchmark lo
         +gic lives in an importable module `app/evaluation/`, used by py
         +test **and** callable from code | `POST /api/v1/admin/eval` run
         +s the retrieval-accuracy and latency benchmark in the backgroun
         +d and stores the results, and a Tests tab shows pass rate, p50/
         +p95 latency and tokens/sec. (The web UI never shells out to `py
         +test`. It only calls the evaluation module, which is safe and r
         +ead-only) |                                                    
      48 +| 7 | Enable/disable AI models (e.g. Claude only, no Ollama) | 
         +Provider **registry** driven by env: `ENABLED_LLM_PROVIDERS=oll
         +ama,anthropic`, `DEFAULT_LLM_PROVIDER`, `EMBEDDING_PROVIDER`. `
         +GET /api/v1/providers` reports which are enabled and healthy. T
         +he index cache key already includes the embedding model name (`
         +storage.md`) | A runtime toggle in the admin area (persisted in
         + SQLite `settings`) and a per-request `options.provider`. **Imp
         +ortant**: "Claude only" also means *no Ollama for embeddings*. 
         +Anthropic has no embeddings API, so we add an alternative `Embe
         +ddingProvider`: **in-process** (`sentence-transformers`/`fastem
         +bed` running the same `embeddinggemma` model, no Ollama contain
         +er) or **Voyage AI** (API, Anthropic's recommended partner). Ol
         +lama becomes an optional Docker Compose **profile** (`docker co
         +mpose --profile ollama up`) |

      50 +### Planned project structure (supports all of the above)

      111 +### Addendum (2026-09-25): impact of `ideas.md`               
      112 +                                                              
      113 +Idea #7 ("work only with Claude, stop using Ollama") changes o
          +ne earlier assumption. Embeddings are *by default* produced by
          + Ollama, but they sit behind their own `EmbeddingProvider` int
          +erface, so a non-Ollama option (in-process `embeddinggemma` vi
          +a sentence-transformers, or Voyage AI) can be added later. The
          + Ollama container becomes an optional Docker Compose profile. 
          +LLM providers must also return **token usage**, which the futu
          +re costs tab needs. See `ideas.md` → "Future-readiness design"
          +.

      49  5. **Support Tiers, SLAs & Escalation Process** (P1–P4 definiti
          ons, response times, when to hand off to a human)
      50  
      51  These deliberately overlap a little (e.g. rate limits appear in
           both the API and the webhooks docs), so that retrieval has to 
          rank sources and the answers can cite more than one document.
      52 +                                                               
      53 +### Addendum (2026-09-25): operational data for future ideas (`
         +ideas.md`)                                                     
      54 +                                                               
      55 +The vector store holds **derived** data only, and it can always
         + be rebuilt from `data/kb/`. The future stats, costs, response-
         +history and runtime-settings features need **source-of-truth** 
         +data that must survive restarts. That will live in a separate *
         +*SQLite** database (a single file on a Docker volume, no extra 
         +container). It will be accessed through repository interfaces (
         +`EventStore`, `HistoryRepository`), so it can move to Postgres 
         +later. If pgvector is ever adopted, the same Postgres instance 
         +can hold both.                                                 
      56 +- **In the MVP**: only the `EventStore` interface, with a log-o
         +nly implementation.                                            
      57 +- **Not in the MVP**: the SQLite database, schema, and migratio
         +ns. They are added with the first idea that needs them.        
      58 +- **Also stays compatible**: the index cache key includes the e
         +mbedding model name, so switching embedding providers (idea #7)
         + automatically triggers a clean re-index.

● The design now leaves room for all 7 ideas without building any of them. I
  added a "Future-readiness design" section to ideas.md that maps each idea to
  the parts that must exist from the start and the parts that come later. None
  of the ideas are marked done, since none are implemented yet.

  What the first version will include, because it's cheap now and saves 
  refactoring later:
  - Full result for every chat turn: besides the answer and citations, each
    response carries the provider, model, token usage, timings, the best
    relevance score and whether it refused. For now this is only logged; the
    stats, costs, history and tests tabs will later store and read it.
  - An API that can grow: the routes are versioned (/api/v1/...), and requests
    take an optional options field for language, detail level and model choice.
    Every response has a conversation id and a message id, which history will
    need.
  - Chat models and embeddings behind separate, configurable interfaces. This
    matters for idea #7 ("Claude only, no Ollama"). Anthropic has no embeddings
    API, so turning Ollama off means the embeddings have to come from somewhere
    else. That can be the same embeddinggemma model running inside the backend,
    or Voyage AI. Ollama becomes an optional part of the Docker Compose setup.
  - Prompts as template files, so the concise and detailed styles (idea #3) are
    just two templates.
  - A test question set stored as a data file, with the evaluation code in its
    own module. pytest uses it now, and a hidden tests tab can run it later
    without starting pytest from the web.

  Left for later:
  - A SQLite database for stats, costs, history and settings. I've added it as a
    note in storage.md, kept separate from the vector index, which can always
    be rebuilt.
  - The admin endpoints, protected by an ADMIN_TOKEN setting. Hiding a menu
    doesn't make it secure, so the token is what protects the data.
  - The hidden admin screens in the frontend.

  I also added a short note to research.md about the embeddings change and a
  planned project structure. CLAUDE.md now says "keep the extension points,
  don't implement the ideas", and I saved the same rule as a memory for future
  sessions. Nothing is committed yet.

● I've added a 10-phase build plan (Phases 0–9) to
  documentation/taskdocs/steps.md, and it's ready for you to check. I also
  marked the four task-doc steps (goal, research, storage, ideas) as ✅. I
  haven't started any code and haven't committed anything.

  The phases
  0. Project skeleton and tools: folders, uv/ruff/pytest, .env.example, and the
  build and test commands added to CLAUDE.md.
  1. Mock knowledge base: the 5 OmniCorp articles, plus about 15 test questions
     with their expected source documents. About 3 of them are questions the
     docs can't answer, to check that the bot refuses.
  2. Configuration and model connections: separate, switchable connections for
     Ollama and Claude (the Anthropic API), with chat models and embeddings kept
     apart.
  3. Loading the documents: splitting them into chunks, the in-memory vector
     store, and saving it to disk. Target: the right document appears in the
     search results for at least 80% of the test questions.
  4. Question-answering pipeline and prompts: citations, refusing politely when
     the answer isn't in the docs, and the full result for every chat turn.
  5. Backend API: /api/v1 with a normal JSON chat endpoint and a streaming one
     (SSE), plus health and provider-status checks.
  6. Frontend: chat screen with streamed answers and citation cards, plus an
     empty /admin page reserved for the future hidden tabs.
  7. Docker Compose: one setup that runs everything locally, including Ollama,
     and one that uses Claude with an API key.
  8. Tests and measurements: checks on search accuracy, refusals and response
     speed, plus optional CI.
  9. README and AI logs: the README sections the brief requires, and the Claude
     transcripts exported to Markdown (mandatory).

  Each phase has "Done when" checks. After each phase I'll mark it ✅, commit,
  and stop for your review.

  Please confirm or change these before I start Phase 0:
  1. Phase 1 content: are the 5 article topics OK? You'll review the articles
     themselves at the end of Phase 1.
  2. Ollama: should I install it on this machine for development, or run it only
     through Docker?
  3. Git: may I commit the documentation changes made so far (CLAUDE.md and the
     task docs), and should I work directly on main?
  4. CI: do you want the GitHub Actions workflow in Phase 8, or should I skip
     it?

  The 5 articles are in backend/data/kb/, about 750–920 words each. Each has
  front matter (id, title, product, lang, updated, tags) and ##/### sections
  that the chunker will use.

  ┌────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   ID   │       Title        │                Main facts                 │

  │        │ Support Tiers,     │ P1–P4 priorities, response times, uptime  │
  │ kb-005 │ SLAs & Escalation  │ credits, escalation path, handing off to  │
  │        │                    │ a human when the KB has no answer         │
  └────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The articles point to each other (for example, an SSO lockout is a P1 ticket,
  and Salesforce syncs use up the API rate limit). The facts are consistent
  across all five, which I checked by hand, so questions that need two documents
  have one correct answer.

  The questions are in backend/tests/eval/questions.yaml:
  - 10 questions answered by a single document.
  - 4 questions that need two documents.
  - 1 question in German, to test multi-language search over the English docs.
  - 3 questions the docs don't answer: pricing, LDAP, and the mobile app's
    offline mode. The bot must refuse politely on these.
  - Each question lists its expected documents and the key facts a correct
    answer should contain.

  Please review:
  1. Is the content realistic enough, and is anything you want missing?
  2. Some questions are deliberately tricky. For example, q13 asks about data
     deleted two months ago. The correct answer is "no longer restorable",
     because the Trash keeps items for 30 days and backups for 35.

● The setup works, but generation is very slow (~2.5 tokens/s) since the VM's
  virtual CPU lacks AVX instructions, masking the host's vector capabilities.

● The reference changes two things: I'll default to claude-opus-5 instead of
  claude-sonnet-5 (overridable via ANTHROPIC_MODEL), and since Opus/Sonnet 5
  reject the temperature parameter and Opus 5 can refuse requests via safety
  filters, I'll skip sending temperature and enable server-side refusal fallback
  by default (toggleable with ANTHROPIC_REFUSAL_FALLBACK=false).

      111  ### Addendum (2026-09-25): impact of `ideas.md`
      112  
      113  Idea #7 ("work only with Claude, stop using Ollama") changes o
           ne earlier assumption. Embeddings are *by default* produced by
            Ollama, but they sit behind their own `EmbeddingProvider` int
           erface, so a non-Ollama option (in-process `embeddinggemma` vi
           a sentence-transformers, or Voyage AI) can be added later. The
            Ollama container becomes an optional Docker Compose profile. 
           LLM providers must also return **token usage**, which the futu
           re costs tab needs. See `ideas.md` → "Future-readiness design"
           .
      114 +                                                              
      115 +### Addendum (2026-09-25): findings from implementation (Phase
          + 2)                                                           
      116 +                                                              
      117 +- **Claude default model is now `claude-opus-5`** (instead of 
          +`claude-sonnet-5`), following Anthropic's current guidance to 
          +default to Opus 5 unless a model is chosen explicitly. `ANTHRO
          +PIC_MODEL` still switches it (e.g. `claude-sonnet-5` is cheape
          +r, `claude-haiku-4-5` is the cheapest).                       
      118 +- **The Anthropic provider sends no `temperature`.** Opus 5 an
          +d Sonnet 5 reject sampling parameters. Answer style is control
          +led by the prompt, and latency and cost by the optional `ANTHR
          +OPIC_EFFORT`.                                                 
      119 +- **Refusal fallback is on by default.** Opus 5 can decline so
          +me requests through its safety classifiers (HTTP 200, `stop_re
          +ason: "refusal"`). The request uses `fallbacks: "default"` (be
          +ta `server-side-fallback-2026-07-01`), which re-runs a decline
          +d request on Anthropic's recommended fallback model. It can be
          + switched off with `ANTHROPIC_REFUSAL_FALLBACK=false`.        
      120 +- **Prompt caching**: the system prompt is marked `cache_contr
          +ol: ephemeral`, and cache hits are reported in `usage.cache_re
          +ad_input_tokens` (relevant for the costs tab, ideas.md #2).   
      121 +- **CPU performance on the dev VM is much lower than expected.
          +** The VM's virtual CPU is "QEMU Virtual CPU version 2.5+" wit
          +h no AVX flags, so llama.cpp falls back to slow scalar code. M
          +easured with `gemma3:4b`: about 2.5 tokens/s generation and 4–
          +13 tokens/s prompt processing. A RAG prompt of about 1,500 tok
          +ens would take minutes. Options:                              
      122 +  1. expose the host CPU to the VM (e.g. `cpu: host` in Proxmo
          +x/libvirt), which is expected to be 5–10× faster              
      123 +  2. use a smaller model (`gemma3:1b`, `qwen3:1.7b`) and fewer
          + or shorter chunks                                            
      124 +  3. use Claude for generation and keep Ollama only for embedd
          +ings (embedding one query takes about 0.1 s even here)

● The Docker setup works, and Phase 2 is done and committed on the new dev
  branch. Following the plan, I'm stopping here for your review before Phase 3.
  There's also a speed problem you need to decide on first (point 3).

  1. Docker Compose setup: working. docker compose up -d --build starts four
  services:
  - ollama: the model server. On first run, a one-off helper (ollama-init)
    downloads gemma3:4b and embeddinggemma.
  - backend: FastAPI with a health check at /api/v1/health.
  - frontend: a placeholder React page (no chat yet), served by nginx, which
    forwards /api calls to the backend. It shows the backend status at
    http://localhost:8080.

  I checked all the connections in the browser path, and /admin also loads the
  app. To make Compose work I built the health endpoint (planned for Phase 5),
  the frontend skeleton (Phase 6) and most of Phase 7 early. Phase 7 is marked
  as partly done in steps.md.

  2. Phase 2 (settings and model connections): done.
  - Switchable connections: Ollama chat, Ollama embeddings and Claude each sit
    behind a common interface. A registry picks them from the settings and
    rejects any provider that is disabled or unknown.
  - Usage and cost data: every answer streams back and ends with token counts
    and the reason it stopped. The future stats and costs tabs need this.
  - Tests: 34 offline tests pass, plus 2 tests against the real Ollama. The real
    embeddings ranked a backup question closest to the backup text, as
    expected.
  - Bug fixed: the tests caught one bug. Claude's "overloaded" error (HTTP 529)
    was being reported as a permanent failure instead of a retryable one.

  Changes to the Claude settings, based on Anthropic's current API guidance:
  - The default model is now claude-opus-5 instead of claude-sonnet-5. Change
    ANTHROPIC_MODEL if you'd rather use Sonnet, which is cheaper.
  - Temperature isn't sent, because Opus 5 and Sonnet 5 no longer accept it.
  - I've turned on Anthropic's server-side refusal fallback: if Opus 5's safety
    filters decline a request, it's re-run on another model. Switch it off with
    ANTHROPIC_REFUSAL_FALLBACK=false.
  - The system prompt is cached, which lowers the cost of repeated questions.

  3. ⚠️ Local generation is too slow on this machine. It produces about 2.5
  tokens per second and reads the prompt at 4–13 tokens per second. The cause is
  that the VM's virtual CPU ("QEMU Virtual CPU") hides the host's AVX
  instructions, so Ollama falls back to slow code. A normal RAG prompt would
  take several minutes per answer. Embeddings are fast enough, at about 0.1 s
  per question. How do you want to handle it?
  - a) Change the VM's CPU type to "host" (in Proxmox/libvirt/QEMU) so the real
    CPU instructions are visible. I'd expect this to be 5–10× faster, and it's
    the best fix if you can do it.
  - b) Switch to a smaller model (gemma3:1b) and put fewer chunks in the prompt.
    This is faster but gives weaker answers.
  - c) Use Claude for the answers, which needs your ANTHROPIC_API_KEY in .env,
    and keep Ollama only for embeddings.

  This choice doesn't block Phase 3 (document loading, chunking, the vector
  store and tuning MIN_SCORE), because Phase 3 only needs embeddings. It does
  matter for Phase 4. I've recorded all of this in research.md.

  Nothing has been pushed yet. Should I start Phase 3?

● Retrieval performs well (mean recall 0.97, hit rate 1.00 at k=5), but
  embedding is slow (5 min for 49 chunks, ~1s/query) and the similarity
  threshold doesn't cleanly separate unanswerable questions—an LDAP question
  scores higher (0.516) than the weakest answerable one (0.416). I'll test some
  clearly off-topic questions to tune the threshold.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed off-topic questions score 0.03–0.18 while answerable ones score
  ≥0.416, so I'll keep MIN_SCORE=0.35 for filtering, relying on the Phase 4
  prompt to catch near-topic but unanswered questions like LDAP or offline mode.
  Now adding the benchmark as a pytest eval test.

      59 +                                                               
      60 +### Results (2026-09-25, Phase 3 implemented)                  
      61 +                                                               
      62 +- **Chunking**: the 5 articles give **49 chunks** (32–147 words
         + each). Every section fits in one window, so the chunk boundari
         +es follow the headings exactly. The section breadcrumb (e.g. `C
         +onfiguring SAML 2.0 › Certificate rotation`) is embedded togeth
         +er with the text and shown in citations.                       
      63 +- **Cache format**: `data/index/vectors.npy` + `chunks.json` + 
         +`manifest.json`. The manifest holds the fingerprint (docs + chu
         +nking parameters + embedding model + format version) and is wri
         +tten last. The index is built in a temporary directory and swap
         +ped in, so a crash never leaves a half-written index that looks
         + valid. Loading the cache takes **~6 ms**. The first build take
         +s **~5 minutes** on the dev VM (slow, AVX-less CPU; see `resear
         +ch.md`), which is why the cache is essential here.             
      64 +- **Retrieval quality** (`embeddinggemma`, k=5, 15 answerable q
         +uestions): **mean recall 0.97, hit rate 1.00**. The only miss i
         +s q13: the support-process document (kb-005) was not in the top
         + 5 for a restore question. This could be improved later with hy
         +brid (BM25 + vector) retrieval.                                
      65 +- **MIN_SCORE calibration** (cosine similarity of the best chun
         +k):                                                            
      66 +                                                               
      67 +  | Question type | Top score |                                
      68 +  |---|---|                                                    
      69 +  | Answerable (15) | 0.416 – 0.746 (median 0.63) |            
      70 +  | Clearly off-topic (weather, jokes, football) | 0.03 – 0.18 
         +|                                                              
      71 +  | Near-topic but not in the KB (pricing, LDAP, mobile offline
         + mode) | 0.32 – 0.52 |                                         
      72 +                                                               
      73 +  **MIN_SCORE stays at 0.35.** It rejects off-topic questions w
         +ithout an LLM call and keeps a margin below the weakest answera
         +ble question. Near-topic unanswerable questions overlap with an
         +swerable scores, so **no threshold can separate them**. The Pha
         +se 4 prompt must make the LLM refuse when the retrieved context
         + doesn't contain the answer, and the Phase 4/8 evaluation measu
         +res that.

      121  - **CPU performance on the dev VM is much lower than expected.
           ** The VM's virtual CPU is "QEMU Virtual CPU version 2.5+" wit
           h no AVX flags, so llama.cpp falls back to slow scalar code. M
           easured with `gemma3:4b`: about 2.5 tokens/s generation and 4–
           13 tokens/s prompt processing. A RAG prompt of about 1,500 tok
           ens would take minutes. Options:
      122    1. expose the host CPU to the VM (e.g. `cpu: host` in Proxmo
           x/libvirt), which is expected to be 5–10× faster
      123    2. use a smaller model (`gemma3:1b`, `qwen3:1.7b`) and fewer
            or shorter chunks

          -ings (embedding one query takes about 0.1 s even here)

● I pushed Phase 2 to origin/dev. Phase 3 is finished and committed locally but
  not pushed yet, so tell me if you want it pushed too. Retrieval passed with a
  large margin: using the real embeddinggemma embeddings, the expected documents
  came back for almost every test question.

  What was built:
  - Chunking: the articles are split by heading, and long sections are packed
    into windows of about 400 tokens with overlap. The 5 articles give 49
    chunks, and every section fits in one. Each chunk keeps its heading path
    (e.g. Configuring SAML 2.0 › Certificate rotation) for the citations.
  - Vector store: an exact in-memory search behind a common interface, so
    pgvector could replace it later.
  - Index cache on disk: it's rebuilt automatically when a document, the
    chunking settings or the embedding model change. It's written so that a
    crash can't leave a broken index. Loading it takes 6 ms, compared with 5
    minutes to build it on this machine.
  - Retrieval benchmark: a reusable module that the future hidden Tests tab can
    call. Run it with uv run python -m app.evaluation.retrieval or pytest -m 
    eval.
  - Tests: 58 offline tests and 4 benchmark tests pass; lint and type checks are
    clean.

  Results with the real embeddings (top 5 results per question):

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │               Metric                │       Result       │   Target    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Share of expected documents found   │ 0.97               │ ≥ 0.8       │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Questions with at least one         │ 1.00               │ —           │
  │ expected document found             │                    │             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Off-topic questions rejected by     │ 3 of 3 (scores     │ —           │
  │ MIN_SCORE=0.35                      │ 0.03–0.18)         │             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Weakest answerable question         │ 0.416              │ stays above │
  │                                     │                    │  0.35       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  Two findings that matter for Phase 4:
  1. The similarity threshold can't catch questions that are near a topic but 
     not answered by the docs. LDAP (0.52) and mobile offline mode (0.45) score
     as high as real answers. The threshold still rejects clearly off-topic
     questions cheaply, but for these cases the answer prompt must tell the
     model to refuse when the retrieved text doesn't contain the answer.
  2. Correction: earlier I said embeddings take about 0.1 s here. That was
     wrong. It's about 0.7–1 s per question and about 6 s per chunk when
     building the index. I've corrected research.md.

  The decision for Phase 4 is still open. Local answers run at about 2.5
  tokens/s, so a full answer would take minutes. The options are the same as
  before:
  - a) change the VM's CPU type to "host"
  - b) use a smaller model
  - c) use Claude for the answers, which needs your ANTHROPIC_API_KEY in .env

  The Phase 4 code works with any of them. To test it end to end, though, I need
  either (a) or an API key for (c), or I'll run with a much smaller local
  model. Which do you prefer?

  What Phase 4 does:
  - How a question is answered: the pipeline finds the relevant chunks and drops
    any below MIN_SCORE. If nothing relevant remains, it replies with a polite
    "not in the knowledge base, ask an expert" message without calling the 
    model. Otherwise the model gets the numbered sources and streams its answer,
    and the [n] markers become citations.
  - Prompts live in app/prompts/system.md and no_answer.md. The rules: answer
    only from the sources, cite every fact, refuse without citations when the
    answer isn't there, stay professional and concise, reply in the language of
    the question, and ignore any instructions inside the sources.
  - How refusals are detected: an answer with no citations counts as a refusal.
    So does a safety-filter decline from Claude. This works whatever language
    the reply is in.
  - Per-turn data: every turn returns a full result with token usage, timings,
    the relevance score and the refusal reason, and a hook records it once. For
    now the hook only writes metrics to the log, not the question text.
  - Tests: 75 offline tests pass; lint and type checks are clean.

  What the local Ollama run showed: a single question timed out after 5 minutes,
  because this CPU couldn't read the roughly 1,500-token prompt within the
  backend's 300-second read timeout. I fixed two things:
  - the error now says it timed out, instead of the misleading "Ollama is
    unreachable"
  - the timeout is configurable with OLLAMA_TIMEOUT_S

  This confirms that local answers aren't usable until you change the VM's CPU
  type. I've saved a memory note so I re-run the Ollama measurements after that
  change.

  Phase 3 and 4 are committed locally only, not pushed. Should I push them along
  with the evaluation results?

         +|                                                              
      70 +| 3 | TOP_K=6 | **18/18** | the response-time table was ranked 
         +6th for the two-document question q12 |                        
      71 +                                                               
      72 +Final run: median 4.8 s per answer, first token after about 2–3
         + s, about 1,300 input and 270 output tokens per answer. Prompt 
         +caching reads about 40% of the input tokens from the cache. The
         + local Ollama run timed out after 300 s on the AVX-less VM, so 
         +it must be re-run after the VM CPU change.                     
      73  - `prompts/system.md`: professional, concise answers, using **o
          nly** the provided context. Sources are cited as `[n]`. The ans
          wer is in the language of the question. If the context doesn't 
          contain the answer, it says so politely and suggests escalating
           to a human (Support/SME).
      74  - `rag/pipeline.py`: embed the question → retrieve top-k → if t
          he best score is below `MIN_SCORE`, refuse without calling the 
          LLM (saves cost and prevents hallucination) → build the prompt 
          → generate → map the `[n]` markers to citations. It returns a `
          ChatResult` with `answer`, `citations`, `language`, `provider`,
           `model`, `usage`, `timings`, `top_score`, `refused`.
      75  - `stores/events/`: the `EventStore` interface plus a log-only 
          implementation, called once per turn.

● Found two issues in the middleware: unsafe string-built log lines that break
  JSON on quoted paths, and the request id being cleared before streamed
  responses finish. I'll replace it with an ASGI middleware that keeps the
  request id set until the response completes.

● Found a real bug: AppState.load retries indefinitely on any error, so config
  problems (like an unregistered provider) leave the API stuck loading instead
  of failing with a clear message. I'll fix it to only retry temporary errors
  and fail fast on configuration errors.

● The startup error handling worked correctly, but it revealed a real bug: the
  index build renames from a temp folder into the mounted data/index volume,
  which fails cross-filesystem (EXDEV) in Docker. I'll fix this by building
  inside data/index/.building and using atomic os.replace to move files into
  place, writing the manifest last.

● Phase 5 (the backend API) is finished and verified end to end in Docker
  through nginx. It's committed as ff3fcf2 but not pushed yet. Earlier phases
  are pushed.

  Endpoints (Swagger documentation at http://localhost:8080/api/v1/... and /docs
  on the backend):

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Endpoint       │                   What it does                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                      │ Always returns 200, with status starting / ok /  │
  │ GET /api/v1/health   │ degraded, whether the index is ready, and the    │
  │                      │ Ollama status                                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GET                  │ Lists the enabled models and whether each is     │
  │ /api/v1/providers    │ usable. Without a key, Claude is shown as        │
  │                      │ unavailable with the reason                      │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                      │ {message, conversation_id?, options:             │
  │ POST /api/v1/chat    │ {provider?}} → answer, citations, whether it     │
  │                      │ refused and why, token usage, timings            │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ POST                 │ The same answer as a stream of events: meta (ids │
  │ /api/v1/chat/stream  │  and candidate sources) → token… → done (full    │
  │                      │ result), or error                                │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  How it behaves:
  - Errors: every error has the same shape, {"error": {"code", "message"}}. A
    bad request returns 422, an unknown or disabled provider 400, an index
    that's still loading or a provider that's down 503, and a provider
    error 502.
  - Unsupported options: anything other than provider inside options (for
    example language) is rejected with 422 rather than silently ignored.
    Language and detail options can be added later without breaking existing
    clients.
  - Startup: the index loads in the background, so the API answers immediately
    with starting / 503 until it's ready.
  - Logs: one JSON line per request, tagged with a request id. The chat metrics
    carry the same id.
  - Tests: 95 offline tests pass, 20 of them new API tests.

  Verified in Docker:
  - The index was built on the volume (~5 minutes on this CPU), and after a
    restart it loaded from the cache within seconds.
  - Claude answered through /chat in 5.3 s with 3 citations.
  - A German question through /chat/stream was answered in German, with the
    first token after 2.3 s.

  Two real bugs found and fixed along the way:
  1. The index couldn't be saved in Docker. It was built in a temporary folder
     next to data/index and then renamed into place. In Docker, data/index is a
     mounted volume, so the rename crossed filesystems and failed, wasting the
     5-minute build. The build now happens inside the index folder. The unit
     tests couldn't catch this because they run on a single filesystem; a new
     test now checks where the files are moved.
  2. A configuration error at startup hung the API. An unknown provider or a
     missing KB folder was retried forever, leaving the API stuck on "loading".
     Now only temporary errors (such as Ollama still starting) are retried, and
     configuration errors are reported in /health straight away.

  Also worth knowing:
  - Claude's answers include some extra detail. For example, the rate-limit
    question also got burst limits and the API Boost add-on. That's within the
    prompt's rules, and "more detail only on request" is idea #3 in ideas.md, so
    I've left it for now.
  - Your .env still has TOP_K=5, which overrides the new default of 6.

  Should I push Phase 5 and move on to Phase 6 (the chat frontend)?

  1. Conversation logs (fa38a3c)
  - scripts/export_ai_logs.py turns Claude Code's session transcripts into
    readable Markdown in documentation/ai-logs/:
    - your prompts and my replies in full
    - tool calls folded away and shortened
    - internal reasoning left out
    - API keys and tokens replaced with [REDACTED]
  - Two hooks in .claude/settings.json run it automatically: after every reply I

  - I checked the files: your real API key doesn't appear in them.
  - ⚠️ One action for you: the .claude/ folder didn't exist when this session
    started, so Claude Code may not have loaded the hooks yet. Open /hooks once,
    or restart Claude Code, to activate them. Until then I re-export the logs
    by hand before every commit, and I've saved that as a standing rule.

  2. Phase 6: the chat UI (3c51813)
  - Chat:
    - answers stream in word by word and render formatting such as bold, lists
      and code
    - each [n] becomes a small numbered chip; clicking it highlights and opens
      the matching source card
    - each source card shows the document, section, a snippet and a match score
  - States: a refusal is shown in amber ("Not covered by the knowledge base").
    There are also clear states for "searching", "writing the answer", errors
    with Try again, and "knowledge base loading". A status indicator in the
    header shows whether the knowledge base is ready.
  - Controls:
    - a Model selector listing the available providers, which lets you use
      Claude while Ollama is slow
    - New conversation
    - example questions to click
    - an empty /admin page reserved for the future hidden tabs
  - Checks: 14 frontend tests, eslint and the type check are clean. All 96
    backend tests pass.
  - Browser test: I ran a real headless browser against the Docker stack:
    - an English answer with 8 citation chips
    - an off-topic question refused in 0.7 s
    - a German question answered in German
    - "New conversation" and /admin both worked
    - no console errors
  - Snippet fix: the source snippets showed raw Markdown (**, backticks, table
    pipes). They now come back from the API as plain text.

  Worth knowing: Claude's answers tend to add "additional details" beyond the
  direct answer. That's within the current prompt rules, and "more detail only
  on request" is idea #3 in ideas.md.

  After the test I removed the test stack with its volumes (the 4 GB model copy)
  and deleted the clone, which held a copy of your key. Your main stack is
  running again, using its cached index.

  What changed in this phase:
  - Pinned images: ollama/ollama:0.34.4, uv:0.12, nginx:stable-alpine, instead
    of latest.
  - Ollama always runs. The optional profile was a trap: without
    COMPOSE_PROFILES in .env, Ollama didn't start, and the backend couldn't
    build its index because embeddings always need Ollama. Now plain docker 
    compose up works even without a .env.
  - Health and port: a frontend healthcheck, and the web port can be set with
    FRONTEND_PORT (default 8080).
  - nginx: security headers including a Content Security Policy, gzip (the JS
    bundle drops from 386 KB to 139 KB), year-long caching for the hashed build
    files, and the request id passed through to the backend.
  - Bug fixed: after a rebuild, compose had kept the backend on its old image. I
    forced a recreate, and it loaded the cached index in seconds.

  Two small things in your .env (I don't edit it, because it contains your key):
  - delete COMPOSE_PROFILES=ollama; it's no longer used
  - set TOP_K=6 if you haven't already

  The remaining phases are 8 (tests and the speed/quality baseline, plus the
  optional CI) and 9 (the README and final log export). Should I push Phase 7
  and continue with Phase 8? And do you want the GitHub Actions CI workflow in
  Phase 8?

      22 +## Future-readiness design (added 2026-09-25)                  
      23 +                                                               
      24 +> **Status**: none of the ideas above are implemented yet, and 
         +none are marked done. This section records the **seams** that t
         +he first version (MVP) of the code, storage and project structu
         +re must include, so that each idea can be added later **without
         + refactoring**. Each seam is marked **MVP** (build it now, chea
         +ply) or **Later** (only reserve room for it).                  
      25 +                                                               
      26 +### Cross-cutting design rules for the MVP                     
      27 +                                                               
      28 +1. **Every chat turn produces a structured result, not just tex
         +t.** The RAG pipeline returns a `ChatResult` containing `answer
         +`, `citations`, `language`, `provider`, `model`, `usage` (input
         +/output tokens), `timings` (retrieval_ms, time-to-first-token, 
         +total_ms), `top_score`, and `refused` (bool). The MVP only logs
         + it. Stats, costs, history and performance tabs will later just
         + *persist and read* it.                                        
      29 +2. **The API is versioned and forward-compatible.** Routes live
         + under `/api/v1/…`. `ChatRequest` = `{ message, conversation_id
         +?, options?: { language?, detail?, provider? } }`. Optional fie
         +lds can be added later without breaking clients. Responses alwa
         +ys carry `conversation_id` and `message_id`.                   
      30 +3. **Two separate provider interfaces**: `LLMProvider` (chat) a
         +nd `EmbeddingProvider` (vectors), each resolved through a **reg
         +istry** from configuration. They are never imported directly by
         + the pipeline.                                                 
      31 +4. **Two separate kinds of storage**:                          
      32 +   - the **vector index** (derived data, rebuildable, in-memory
         + + npz cache; see `storage.md`)                                
      33 +   - an **operational database** (source of truth for events, h
         +istory, costs), which is **added later** as SQLite behind a rep
         +ository interface.                                             
      34 +5. **The "hidden" UI is a real admin area, not just hidden.**  
      35 +   - Backend: all admin endpoints under `/api/v1/admin/*`, guar
         +ded by an `ADMIN_TOKEN` env var (disabled when unset).         
      36 +   - Frontend: an `/admin` route opened by a keyboard shortcut.
         + Hiding a menu is not security, so the token is what protects t
         +he data.                                                       
      37 +                                                               
      38 +### Idea → seam mapping                                        
      39 +                                                               
      40 +| # | Idea | What the MVP must already have | What gets added l
         +ater |                                                         
      41 +|---|---|---|---|                                              
      42 +| 1 | Hidden statistics menu | `ChatResult` metrics (rule 1). A
         + single `record_event(result)` hook called after every turn, a 
         +no-op/log-only implementation in the MVP | SQLite `chat_events`
         + table plus `/api/v1/admin/stats` (questions/day, refusal rate,
         + avg latency, top cited docs, provider split) and a Stats tab |
      43 +| 2 | Hidden costs menu + optimisation | Providers **return tok
         +en usage**: Anthropic `usage.input_tokens/output_tokens`, Ollam
         +a `prompt_eval_count/eval_count`. `usage` is included in `ChatR
         +esult` | A pricing table in config (`$ per MTok` per model; Oll
         +ama = $0 or an estimated compute cost), a Costs tab, and optimi
         +sations: Anthropic **prompt caching** of the system prompt, few
         +er/shorter chunks (top-k tuning), routing simple questions to H
         +aiku, and a **semantic answer cache** (reuse the `VectorStore` 
         +interface on past questions) |                                 
      44 +| 3 | Professional, concise answers; detail on request | Prompt
         +s live in **versioned template files** (`backend/app/prompts/*.
         +md`), not inline strings. The system prompt already enforces a 
         +professional, concise, cited style | An `options.detail = "conc
         +ise" \| "detailed"` field chooses the template variant, plus a 
         +"More details" button in the UI that re-asks with `detail=detai
         +led` |                                                         
      45 +| 4 | Q&A in different languages | **Multilingual embedding mod
         +el** (`embeddinggemma`, already chosen), so questions in other 
         +languages still retrieve English docs. `language` field in `Cha
         +tResult`. Chunk metadata includes `lang` | `options.language` (
         +`auto` by default = reply in the question's language), a langua
         +ge selector in the UI, and optionally KB articles in other lang
         +uages (filtered via `lang` metadata) |                         
      46 +| 5 | Hidden response-history tab | `conversation_id` + `messag
         +e_id` in the API from day 1. The frontend keeps the conversatio
         +n in state | SQLite `conversations` + `messages` tables (questi
         +on, answer, citations JSON, provider, timings) via a `HistoryRe
         +pository`, a History tab, and multi-turn context (question rewr
         +iting using earlier turns) |                                   
      47 +| 6 | Hidden tab with unit, speed and performance tests | The *
         +*evaluation set is data**: `backend/tests/eval/questions.yaml` 
         +(question → expected `doc_id`s / must-refuse). The benchmark lo
         +gic lives in an importable module `app/evaluation/`, used by py
         +test **and** callable from code | `POST /api/v1/admin/eval` run
         +s the retrieval-accuracy and latency benchmark in the backgroun
         +d and stores the results, and a Tests tab shows pass rate, p50/
         +p95 latency and tokens/sec. (The web UI never shells out to `py
         +test`. It only calls the evaluation module, which is safe and r
         +ead-only) |                                                    
      48 +| 7 | Enable/disable AI models (e.g. Claude only, no Ollama) | 
         +Provider **registry** driven by env: `ENABLED_LLM_PROVIDERS=oll
         +ama,anthropic`, `LLM_PROVIDER` (default), `EMBEDDING_PROVIDER`.
         + `GET /api/v1/providers` reports which are enabled and healthy.
         + *(Implemented early: a per-request `options.provider` and a mo
         +del selector in the UI.)* The index cache key already includes 
         +the embedding model name (`storage.md`) | A runtime toggle in t
         +he admin area (persisted in SQLite `settings`) and a per-reques
         +t `options.provider`. **Important**: "Claude only" also means *
         +no Ollama for embeddings*. Anthropic has no embeddings API, so 
         +we add an alternative `EmbeddingProvider`: **in-process** (`sen
         +tence-transformers`/`fastembed` running the same `embeddinggemm
         +a` model, no Ollama container) or **Voyage AI** (API, Anthropic
         +'s recommended partner). Ollama becomes an optional Docker Comp
         +ose **profile** (`docker compose --profile ollama up`) |       
      49 +                                                               
      50 +### Planned project structure (supports all of the above)      
      51 +                                                               
      52 +```

  ⎿  Updated documentation/taskdocs/storage.md (+1 -1)
      29  ### Decision: **in-memory vector store + on-disk embedding cach
          e, behind a `VectorStore` interface**
      30  
      31  1. **`InMemoryVectorStore`**: a numpy matrix of L2-normalised e
          mbeddings plus a parallel list of chunk metadata (`doc_id`, `ti
          tle`, `section`, `chunk_id`, `text`). Search is a single matrix
          -vector dot product followed by top-k. A **similarity threshold
          ** is also applied: if the best score is below it, the backend 
          replies that "the documentation doesn't cover this" instead of 
          letting the LLM guess. This is the basis for the polite refusal
           / human handoff feature.
      32 -2. **Embedding cache** (fixes the restart downside): after inge
         -stion, vectors and metadata are saved to `data/index/index.npz`
         - + `meta.json` on a Docker volume. The key is a hash of *(docum
         -ent contents + chunking parameters + embedding model name)*. On
         - startup, if the hash matches, the index loads instantly. Other
         -wise it is re-ingested automatically. Changing a doc or the mod
         -el therefore rebuilds the index, with no stale data.           
      32 +2. **Embedding cache** (fixes the restart downside): after inge
         +stion, vectors and metadata are saved to `data/index/` on a Doc
         +ker volume (implemented as `vectors.npy` + `chunks.json` + `man
         +ifest.json`, see Results). The key is a hash of *(document cont
         +ents + chunking parameters + embedding model name)*. On startup
         +, if the hash matches, the index loads instantly. Otherwise it 
         +is re-ingested automatically. Changing a doc or the model there
         +fore rebuilds the index, with no stale data.                   
      33  3. **Interface** (`add`, `search`, `count`): Chroma or pgvector
           can be swapped in later without touching the RAG pipeline. Thi
          s is the trade-off story for the README and the interview: "exa
          ct in-memory search is the right tool at prototype scale; here 
          is the seam where pgvector would plug in at 100k+ chunks or mul
          ti-replica deployment."
      34  
      35  **Why not Chroma or pgvector now**: they add a container or hea
          vy dependency, startup time and failure modes, but they don't i
          mprove retrieval quality at this size. Brute-force search is *m
          ore* accurate than their ANN indexes. We should move to pgvecto
          r when any of these holds: the corpus grows beyond ~50–100k chu
          nks, several backend replicas need a shared index, or we need i
          ncremental updates and metadata-filtered queries (e.g. per-prod
          uct or per-customer ACLs).

  Fixed during this review:
  1. nginx timeout bug. nginx waited at most 300 s for the backend, exactly the
     backend's own Ollama timeout. With a slow model, nginx could cut the
     connection first, so the UI would say "The answer was interrupted" instead
     of the clear "Ollama did not respond within 300 s" message. nginx now waits
     up to 900 s. I checked the logic but didn't reproduce it, because that
     takes a 5-minute Ollama run.
  2. Outdated docs. Four statements that later decisions overrode are corrected:
     the removed Ollama profile, the old default Claude model, the old index
     file names, and the old name of the provider setting.
